Sometimes called heterologa, the words nātālis, fidēs, tabula, sāl, and rōstrum all have what grammatical oddity in common? Roughly speaking, Supreme Court opinions are printed as follows: the majority opinion; any concurrences, in order of the justices’ seniority; then any dissents, in order of the justices’ seniority. With this in mind, if a concurrence by Justice Thomas were to reference a remark made in a dissent by Justice Kagan, what two-letter abbreviation might be used to point out this location later on in the set of opinions? ANSWER:V.I.  BONUS: V.I., of course, stands for vide infra. ANSWER:VIDE SUPRA  BONUS: Perhaps the two justices’ disagreement is rooted in a dispute over which side in the case bore the burden of proof. What two word Latin phrase means “burden of proof”? ANSWER:ONUS PROBANDI

ANSWER:O -> U AND U -> I  BONUS: Despite Latin’s extremely phonetic spelling, comparing different forms of a particular lemma can reveal some information about the underlying structure of the word. ANSWER:REGS, INFANTS, NOCTS (THEY SHOULD BE SPELLED)  BONUS: For your second bonus, excluding K, J, Y, and Z, give four Latin letters that never appear at the end of a word. ANSWER:ANY FOUR OF F, G, H, P, Q, V (AS DISTINCT FROM U) (B APPEARS AT THE END OF AB)

BONUS: Scan and identify any irregularities in this line from Caelius Sedulius’s Carmen Paschāle: “cuius onus leve est, cuius iuga ferre suave est” ANSWER:DDSDDS; HIATUS AFTER LEVE; SUĀVE IS IRREGULARLY TRISYLLABIC 
Translate into Latin, using a supine and the verb spernō: “With a storm approaching, the sailor hoped that his prayers would not be scorned by the goddesses.” TEMPESTĀTE/PROCELLĀ APPROPINQUANTE, ANSWER:NAUTA SPĒRĀVIT/SPĒRĀBAT PRECĒS (SUĀS) Ā DEĀBUS NŌN SPRĒTUM ĪRĪ BONUS: Now translate using the verb noceō: “Although Neptune was said to hate him, the goddesses of the sea promised that his ship wouldn’t be harmed.” QUAMQUAM/ETSĪ DĪCĒBĀTUR / QUAMVĪS/CUM DĪCERĒTUR NEPTŪNUS EUM ŌDISSE, DEAE MARIS/PELAGĪ/PONTĪ/FRETĪ PRŌMĪSĒRUNT/ ANSWER:POLLICITAE SUNT NŌN FORE UT / FORE UT NŌN / FORE NĒ ĒIUS NĀVĪ NOCĒRĒTUR BONUS: Now translate into good Latin, using a syncopated form: “The helmsman knew the ship would’ve been covered by the waves if the goddesses had not saved him.” GUBERNĀTOR/RĒCTOR SCIĒBAT/SCĪVIT FUTŪRUM FUISSE UT NĀVIS FLŪCTIBUS/UNDĪS OPERĪRĒTUR/OBRUERĒTUR/ ANSWER:MERGERĒTUR/TEGERĒTUR, NISI/SĪ NŌN/NĪ DEAE SĒ SERVĀSSENT 
Give two archaic verb forms to complete this unusual analogy: negāvī : negāssim :: sunt : [BLANK]  ANSWER:SIENT AND FUANT BONUS: Give all possible verb forms that could complete this analogy, in which both verbs are sometimes irregular: edō : edit :: cōnficimur : [BLANK]  ANSWER:CŌNFICIUNTUR, CŌNFĪUNT, CŌNFICIANTUR, AND CŌNFĪANT BONUS: Give all possible verb forms that could complete this highly complicated analogy: dēfendit : dēfendēre :: vēnit : [BLANK] ANSWER:VĒNEUNT, VĒNIĒRE/VĒNIĒRUNT, AND VĒNĒRE/VĒNĒRUNT (ACCEPT “VĒNERANT” AS A 4TH OPTION) 
Note: there is an extra clue at the end. Feel free to wait if you are unsure. Translate the following Greek sentence about the character of tyrannical rulers: “ὅτι ἔξεστιν αὐτοῖς πάντα λαμβάνειν, πάντων ἐπιθυμοῦσιν.” The verb ἐπιθυμέω means “to crave.” ANSWER:BECAUSE IT IS POSSIBLE FOR THEM TO TAKE EVERYTHING, THEY CRAVE EVERYTHING BONUS: Now translate this sentence about an ideal ruler: “δικαιότερος δὲ τῶν ἄλλων ἐστίν, ἅτε πᾶσι παρέχων τὴν δικαιοσύνην.” ANSWER:(AND/BUT) HE IS MORE JUST THAN THE OTHERS, BECAUSE HE PROVIDES JUSTICE FOR EVERYONE BONUS: Finally, keeping in mind that θεραπεύω means “to flatter,” translate the following sentence about Alexander the Great: “θεραπευτέον αὐτῷ τούς τ’ ἄρχοντας καὶ τὸν ὄχλον λόγοις τε καὶ δώροις πολλάκις.” ANSWER:MANY TIMES/OFTEN/AGAIN AND AGAIN HE HAD TO FLATTER BOTH THE LEADERS AND THE COMMON PEOPLE/MASSES WITH BOTH WORDS AND GIFTS 
Of the noun forms obice, incūde, trāmite, and fastū, which best fills in the blank in the following sentence? “Nec arguī umquam potuit dēviāsse ab aequitātis rēctō [BLANK].” ANSWER:TRĀMITE BONUS: Of the noun forms obice, canistrō, incūde, fornice, and fastū, which best completes the following sentence? “Post ēius excessum, nōbiliōris mīlitiae [BLANK] ēlātus, in Iūliānum paria cōnfingēbat.” ANSWER:FASTŪ BONUS: The forms that remain come from the nouns obex, canistrum, incūs, and fornix. Define any three of these words. ANSWER:ANY 3 OF: OBEX – BOLT/BAR; CANISTRUM – BASKET; INCŪS – ANVIL; FORNIX – ARCH/BROTHEL  
What use of the subjunctive can be found in the following sentence? “Ut tē illō tempore gesserīs, ut patrimōnium omnibus luxuriae fōrmīs cōnsūmpserīs, supervacāneum est dīcere.” ANSWER:INDIRECT QUESTION BONUS: What use of the subjunctive can be found here? “Scaurus rēgem Cimbrōrum monuit ita Rōmānōs ēius exercitum fūsūrōs ut Hannibalis, ut anteā Brennī cōpiās dēlēvissent.” ANSWER:SUBORDINATE CLAUSE IN INDIRECT DISCOURSE BONUS: What use of the subjunctive can be found in this sentence, spoken by Ariadne in the Hērōides? “Nōn poterant fīgī praecordia ferrea cornū; ut tē nōn tegerēs, pectore tūtus erās.” ANSWER:CONCESSIVE
